PMCID: PMC13316234 PMID: 42381966

### Abstract
Plant-derived nanoparticles (PNPs) have emerged as a promising class of bioactive nanomaterials with intrinsic immunomodulatory properties, offering a sustainable and biocompatible alternative to synthetic delivery systems. However, their systematic exploration has been hindered by a fragmented “one-plant-one-function” research paradigm. Here, we establish a comprehensive bio-discovery platform by constructing a taxonomically diverse library of 110 PNPs. Through integrated physicochemical characterization and high-throughput functional screening, we identify Allium tuberosum -derived nanoparticles (ANPs) as a potent bioactive nanoparticle that effectively promotes pro-inflammatory macrophage polarization. Mechanistically, we demonstrate that the bioactivity of ANPs is largely mediated by their endogenous RNA cargo, specifically ANP-miR-488, which modulates the Bcl6 signaling axis to initiate macrophage reprogramming. Furthermore, ANPs significantly sensitize cold tumors to immune checkpoint blockade therapy in a syngeneic LLC1 lung carcinoma model. This study not only provides a generalizable strategy for the systematic discovery of bioactive nanomaterials but also highlights the clinical potential of RNA-mediated PNPs as a promising platform for enhancing cancer immunotherapy.

### Highlights
• Plant nanoparticle library enables unbiased discovery of bioactive nanomaterials.
• Allium tuberosum nanoparticles induce M1 macrophages to enhance tumor immunity.
• miR-488 of Allium tuberosum nanoparticles reprograms macrophages via Bcl6 regulation.
• Allium tuberosum nanoparticles remodel tumor microenvironment for anti-PD-1 therapy.

### 1. Introduction
Nanomaterials with defined nanoscale architectures have enabled diverse applications in drug delivery, imaging, and immunotherapy [ [1] , [2] , [3] , [4] ]. In synthetic systems, rigorous control over chemical composition, structural geometry, and surface functionalization has enabled the engineering of specific functional properties into nanoscale carriers [ [5] , [6] , [7] ]. However, these design strategies have been largely restricted to engineered synthetic nanomaterials, while naturally occurring nanoscale systems have rarely been systematically explored as bioactive nanomaterial platforms [ 8 , 9 ]. Nature-derived nanomaterials offer a sophisticated alternative where nanoscale organization is shaped by biological evolution, providing structurally complex and highly biocompatible systems with inherent functional potential [ 10 , 11 ].
Among these, plant-derived nanoparticles (PNPs) have recently emerged as a promising class of bioactive nanomaterials, characterized by high biocompatibility, low toxicity, and a diverse repertoire of molecular cargo [ 8 , [12] , [13] , [14] ]. These PNPs are enriched with endogenous lipids, proteins, and nucleic acids and can be sustainably produced at high yields from renewable botanical sources [ 15 , 16 ]. Emerging evidence suggests that PNPs function not only as inert delivery vehicles but also active modulators capable of modulating complex biological processes, including targeted drug delivery [ [17] , [18] , [19] ], inflammation regulation [ [20] , [21] , [22] , [23] ], and cancer therapy [ [24] , [25] , [26] , [27] ].
Despite these rapid advances, current investigations into PNPs remain largely phenomenological and fragmented [ 28 , 29 ]. Most studies focus on a single plant species selected based on empirical or ethnopharmacological knowledge, following a restrictive “one-plant-one-function” paradigm reminiscent of early natural product research [ 30 ]. This conventional approach limits systematic comparison across dive

### 2.1. Library construction and physicochemical mapping of PNPs
To enable systematic discovery of bioactive nanomaterials, we constructed a comprehensive library of PNPs encompassing 110 botanically diverse plant species across 34 taxonomic families ( Fig. 1 A and Table S1 ). Unlike conventional studies focusing on individual plants, we deliberately designed this comprehensive library as a comparative nanomaterial platform to systematically investigate complex structure-function relationships across a diverse range of species. PNPs were isolated from multiple plant tissues, including leaves, aerial parts, fruits, and roots, thereby capturing intrinsic variability in nanoscale architecture and molecular organization ( Fig. S1 ). To ensure that functional differences arise from intrinsic nanoparticle properties rather than isolation artifacts, we implemented a standardized, low-shear extraction and differential centrifugation workflow ( Fig. 1 B). This protocol enabled reproducible fabrication of nanoscale carriers across all plant sources, independent of tissue texture or water content.
Construction and physicochemical characterization of the PNP library . (A) Taxonomic analysis of the 110 plant species comprising the PNP library. The library includes 59 edible plants and 51 medicinal plants, spanning 34 families, with prominent representation from Asteraceae, Brassicaceae, and Fabaceae. Within the library, certain species include distinct cultivars (e.g., Capsicum annuum varieties) or specific plant tissues (e.g., Senna tora seed, stem, leaf). These entries are indicated as extended segments in the outer ring. (B) Schematic overview of the PNP isolation protocol applied uniformly across 110 plant species. The process consists of low-speed cold-press extraction to preserve nanoparticle integrity, followed by purification via differential centrifugation. All centrifugation steps were conducted at 4 °C. (C) Representative transmission electron microscopy (TEM) images of eight PNP species, randomly selected across plant tissue categ

### 2.2. Functional screening as materials discovery
To enable systematic discovery of functional PNPs as active bio-nanomaterials, we employ interferon-γ (IFN-γ) production as a quantitative functional screening readout [ 36 , 37 ] . IFN-γ represents an emergent, integrative output of multicellular immune crosstalk and thus serves as a sensitive proxy for identifying nanomaterials capable of modulating complex biological responses, rather than merely interacting with isolated cell types. Primary murine splenocytes isolated from C57BL/6 mice, which preserve endogenous interactions among macrophages, dendritic cells, and lymphocytes, were employed as a systems-level screening matrix. Each PNP preparation from the 110-species library was evaluated under standardized conditions normalized to particle number to ensure comparability across botanically diverse sources ( Fig. 2 A). This design enabled quantitative mapping of functional performance across the nanoparticle library.
Functional screening identifies ANPs as potent immunomodulatory nanomaterials . (A) Schematic illustration of the ex vivo screening assay using murine splenocytes. (B) Screening of 110 PNPs for IFN-γ induction. Top-performing PNPs (highlighted in blue) were selected for further analysis. (C) IL-10 concentrations in splenocyte supernatants following 24 h treatment (ELISA, n = 3). (D) Biocompatibility of PNPs in human PBMCs assessed by flow cytometry (Live/Dead staining) after 48 h incubation. Data are presented as the percentage of live cells ( n = 4). (E) Frequency of IFN-γ + human PBMCs after 24 h treatment ( n = 4). (F) Comparative analysis of IFN-γ induction by ANPs relative to other Allium -derived nanoparticles. AcNPs, Allium cepa -NPs (onion); AfNPs, Allium fistulosum -NPs (spring onion); AsNPs, Allium sativum -NPs (garlic); AwNPs, Allium wakegi Araki -NPs (green onion). Representative flow cytometry histograms (left) and corresponding quantification (right, n = 3). (G) Representative flow cytometry plots (left) and corresponding quantificatio

### 2.3. ANPs drive M1 macrophage-mediated antitumor efficacy in vivo
To evaluate whether the immunostimulatory signature identified during functional screening translates into a therapeutic response in vivo, we next examined the antitumor performance of ANPs in syngeneic tumor models. In the B16F10 melanoma model, intravenous administration of ANPs resulted in a pronounced suppression of tumor growth compared with controls ( Fig. 3 A and B and Fig. S9 ). Immunophenotypic analysis of the tumor microenvironment revealed that ANPs induced a coordinated remodeling of myeloid and lymphoid compartments. Specifically, ANP treatment significantly increased the proportion of CD11b + macrophages expressing M1-associated markers (CD80 and TNF-α) while concomitantly enhancing the infiltration and effector activation of CD8 + T cells ( Fig. 3 C, Fig. S10, and Fig. S11A ). These effects were not restricted to the tumor site but were also evident in secondary lymphoid organs, including the spleen and tumor-draining lymph nodes ( Fig. 3 D, Fig. S11B and Fig. S11C ), indicating that ANPs act as systemically active immunomodulatory nanomaterials rather than locally confined agents. Immunohistochemical analysis further confirmed broad immune cell recruitment, including CD3 ε + T cells, F4/80+ macrophages, and CD11c + antigen-presenting cells, in ANP-treated tumors ( Fig. S12 ). While proliferative activity, assessed by Ki-67 staining, remained unchanged, cleaved caspase-3 levels were significantly elevated, indicating enhanced apoptotic signaling within the tumor tissue.
In vivo antitumor efficacy of ANPs mediated by macrophage reprogramming . (A) Schematic overview of the experimental design of the syngeneic cancer models. Five days after subcutaneous tumor implantation, PBS (control) or ANPs (2 × 10 11 particles) were administered intravenously. (B) Growth curves of B16F10-luc melanoma tumors in C57BL/6 mice (left) and weights of excised tumors from each group (right, n = 5). (C, D) Flow cytometry analysis of T lymphocytes and macrophages in (C) tumo

### 2.4. ANP-RNA triggers M1 macrophage-mediated immunity via the ANP-miR-488/Bcl6 axis
To more effectively resolve bioactive cargo encoded within ANPs, ultracentrifugation-isolated ANPs were further purified by density gradient purification ( Fig. S20 ). Subsequently, functional contributions of distinct components were dissected at the nanomaterial level by reconstituting DNA, RNA, or metabolite fractions extracted from ANPs into liposomes, while lipid shells were applied directly. Protein-associated effects were examined independently using heat-inactivated ANPs ( Fig. S21 ). Notably, in RAW 264.7 cells, treatment with the isolated RNA induced the most pronounced morphological changes ( Fig. 4 A) and a robust upregulation of M1-associated markers (CD80, CD86, MHC-I, iNOS, and TNF-α) along with IFN-γ, as revealed by flow cytometry ( Fig. 4 B and Fig. S22A ). The preserved bioactivity of heat-inactivated ANPs indicates that the observed immunostimulatory function is not protein-mediated but instead primarily driven by non-protein components, excluding proteins as dominant functional mediators. In addition, treatment with isolated RNA cargo induced a clear dose-dependent increase in IFN-γ and CD80 expression ( Fig. S22B and Fig. S23 ).
RNA cargo mediates the immunomodulatory activity of ANPs . (A) Representative microscopy images of RAW 264.7 macrophages treated with PBS (Con), LPS, ANPs, commercial Lipofectamine 3000 alone (Lipo), ANP‐derived lipid shell (Shell), heat‐inactivated ANPs, ANP‐derived RNA-loaded liposomes (RNA), or metabolite‐loaded liposomes (Metabolite). Arrows indicate cells exhibiting activation-associated morphological changes. Scale bars, 100 μm (B) Representative flow cytometry histograms of CD86, MHC‐I, and TNF‐α expression in RAW 264.7 macrophages treated with individual ANP cargo components. (C) Schematic overview of ANP‐RNA generation. The lipid fraction and total RNA were independently isolated from ANPs and subsequently reconstituted into RNA‐reloaded nanoparticles using a standard encapsulation procedure. (D) RT-qPCR analysi

### 2.5. Clinical relevance and synergistic αPD-1 efficacy of ANPs
To explore the clinical relevance of macrophage state regulation associated with the ANP-miR-488/ BCL6 axis, human single-cell and bulk transcriptomes were integrated. A macrophage-enriched signature was defined using lung adenocarcinoma (LUAD) single-cell data ( GSE131907 ) ( Fig. 6 A). This signature was then applied to LUAD and lung squamous cell carcinoma cohorts to stratify macrophage-enriched patient samples, which were further classified into BCL6 high and BCL6 low groups ( Fig. S34 ). The BCL6 low group exhibited a pronounced immune-inflamed phenotype, characterized by elevated expression of M1-associated inflammatory markers ( TNF, CD80, CD86, CXCL9 , and CXCL10 ), whereas immunosuppressive markers ( ARG1, CD163 ) were reduced or unchanged ( Fig. 6 B). Moreover, CD8 + T cell infiltration ( CD8A, CD8B, CXCR3 ) and effector activation signatures ( IFNG, GZMB, PRF1 ) were markedly upregulated in the BCL6 low group ( Fig. 6 C), recapitulating the macrophage-T cell crosstalk observed in our experimental models.
Clinical relevance of ANPs associated with BCL6-dependent immune remodeling and enhanced response to αPD-1 therapy. (A) Identification of a macrophage-enriched gene signature using the GSE131907 lung adenocarcinoma (LUAD) single-cell RNA-sequencing dataset. (B, C) Transcriptomic analysis of macrophage-enriched TCGA lung cancer cohorts (LUAD and LUSC) stratified into BCL6 high and BCL6 low groups. (B) Violin plots showing the expression of M1-associated inflammatory markers ( TNF , CD80 , CD86 , CXCL9 , CXCL10 ) and immunosuppressive markers ( ARG1 , CD163 ). (C) Violin plots showing genes associated with CD8 + T cell infiltration ( CD8A , CD8B , CXCR3 ) and cytotoxic effector molecules ( IFNG , GZMB , PRF1 ). (D) Association between BCL6 expression and clinical response to αPD-1 therapy in the GSE299686 cohort. Macrophage-enriched patients were stratified by BCL6 expression level, and the proportions of responders versus non-responders were compared. (E) 

### 3. Conclusion
In this study, we establish a systematic discovery framework for bioactive plant-derived nanoparticles (PNPs) that moves beyond the conventional one-plant-one-function paradigm. By constructing and physicochemically mapping a taxonomically diverse library of 110 PNPs, we demonstrate that biological functionality can be quantitatively assessed through functional screening, enabling unbiased identification of functional bio-nanomaterials from natural diversity. Using this approach, ANPs were identified as a previously unrecognized functional PNP with pronounced immunomodulatory activity, emerging from systematic screening rather than predefined molecular design.
Mechanistic analyses revealed that ANP bioactivity is primarily associated with RNA cargo carried by the PNPs. In particular, ANP-miR-488 suppresses the transcriptional regulator Bcl6, establishing a clear structure-function relationship that links nanoscale carrier architecture to immune modulation. These findings suggest that RNA functions as a key active component contributing to the immunomodulatory activity of ANPs.

### Ethics approval and consent to participate
All animal procedures were conducted in accordance with protocols approved by the Kyungpook National University Institutional Animal Care and Use Committee (IACUC; approval number: KNU-2025-0783).
PBMCs from healthy donors were obtained from Kyungpook National University Medical Center (KNUMC). Blood collection was approved by the Institutional Review Board of KNUMC (Approval No. 2022-02-011), and written informed consent was obtained from all donors.

PMCID: PMC13316234 PMID: 42381966

### Abstract
Plant-derived nanoparticles (PNPs) have emerged as a promising class of bioactive nanomaterials with intrinsic immunomodulatory properties, offering a sustainable and biocompatible alternative to synthetic delivery systems. However, their systematic exploration has been hindered by a fragmented “one-plant-one-function” research paradigm. Here, we establish a comprehensive bio-discovery platform by constructing a taxonomically diverse library of 110 PNPs. Through integrated physicochemical characterization and high-throughput functional screening, we identify Allium tuberosum -derived nanoparticles (ANPs) as a potent bioactive nanoparticle that effectively promotes pro-inflammatory macrophage polarization. Mechanistically, we demonstrate that the bioactivity of ANPs is largely mediated by their endogenous RNA cargo, specifically ANP-miR-488, which modulates the Bcl6 signaling axis to initiate macrophage reprogramming. Furthermore, ANPs significantly sensitize cold tumors to immune checkpoint blockade therapy in a syngeneic LLC1 lung carcinoma model. This study not only provides a generalizable strategy for the systematic discovery of bioactive nanomaterials but also highlights the clinical potential of RNA-mediated PNPs as a promising platform for enhancing cancer immunotherapy.

### Highlights
• Plant nanoparticle library enables unbiased discovery of bioactive nanomaterials.
• Allium tuberosum nanoparticles induce M1 macrophages to enhance tumor immunity.
• miR-488 of Allium tuberosum nanoparticles reprograms macrophages via Bcl6 regulation.
• Allium tuberosum nanoparticles remodel tumor microenvironment for anti-PD-1 therapy.

### 1. Introduction
Nanomaterials with defined nanoscale architectures have enabled diverse applications in drug delivery, imaging, and immunotherapy [ [1] , [2] , [3] , [4] ]. In synthetic systems, rigorous control over chemical composition, structural geometry, and surface functionalization has enabled the engineering of specific functional properties into nanoscale carriers [ [5] , [6] , [7] ]. However, these design strategies have been largely restricted to engineered synthetic nanomaterials, while naturally occurring nanoscale systems have rarely been systematically explored as bioactive nanomaterial platforms [ 8 , 9 ]. Nature-derived nanomaterials offer a sophisticated alternative where nanoscale organization is shaped by biological evolution, providing structurally complex and highly biocompatible systems with inherent functional potential [ 10 , 11 ].
Among these, plant-derived nanoparticles (PNPs) have recently emerged as a promising class of bioactive nanomaterials, characterized by high biocompatibility, low toxicity, and a diverse repertoire of molecular cargo [ 8 , [12] , [13] , [14] ]. These PNPs are enriched with endogenous lipids, proteins, and nucleic acids and can be sustainably produced at high yields from renewable botanical sources [ 15 , 16 ]. Emerging evidence suggests that PNPs function not only as inert delivery vehicles but also active modulators capable of modulating complex biological processes, including targeted drug delivery [ [17] , [18] , [19] ], inflammation regulation [ [20] , [21] , [22] , [23] ], and cancer therapy [ [24] , [25] , [26] , [27] ].
Despite these rapid advances, current investigations into PNPs remain largely phenomenological and fragmented [ 28 , 29 ]. Most studies focus on a single plant species selected based on empirical or ethnopharmacological knowledge, following a restrictive “one-plant-one-function” paradigm reminiscent of early natural product research [ 30 ]. This conventional approach limits systematic comparison across dive

### 2.1. Library construction and physicochemical mapping of PNPs
To enable systematic discovery of bioactive nanomaterials, we constructed a comprehensive library of PNPs encompassing 110 botanically diverse plant species across 34 taxonomic families ( Fig. 1 A and Table S1 ). Unlike conventional studies focusing on individual plants, we deliberately designed this comprehensive library as a comparative nanomaterial platform to systematically investigate complex structure-function relationships across a diverse range of species. PNPs were isolated from multiple plant tissues, including leaves, aerial parts, fruits, and roots, thereby capturing intrinsic variability in nanoscale architecture and molecular organization ( Fig. S1 ). To ensure that functional differences arise from intrinsic nanoparticle properties rather than isolation artifacts, we implemented a standardized, low-shear extraction and differential centrifugation workflow ( Fig. 1 B). This protocol enabled reproducible fabrication of nanoscale carriers across all plant sources, independent of tissue texture or water content.
Construction and physicochemical characterization of the PNP library . (A) Taxonomic analysis of the 110 plant species comprising the PNP library. The library includes 59 edible plants and 51 medicinal plants, spanning 34 families, with prominent representation from Asteraceae, Brassicaceae, and Fabaceae. Within the library, certain species include distinct cultivars (e.g., Capsicum annuum varieties) or specific plant tissues (e.g., Senna tora seed, stem, leaf). These entries are indicated as extended segments in the outer ring. (B) Schematic overview of the PNP isolation protocol applied uniformly across 110 plant species. The process consists of low-speed cold-press extraction to preserve nanoparticle integrity, followed by purification via differential centrifugation. All centrifugation steps were conducted at 4 °C. (C) Representative transmission electron microscopy (TEM) images of eight PNP species, randomly selected across plant tissue categ

### 2.2. Functional screening as materials discovery
To enable systematic discovery of functional PNPs as active bio-nanomaterials, we employ interferon-γ (IFN-γ) production as a quantitative functional screening readout [ 36 , 37 ] . IFN-γ represents an emergent, integrative output of multicellular immune crosstalk and thus serves as a sensitive proxy for identifying nanomaterials capable of modulating complex biological responses, rather than merely interacting with isolated cell types. Primary murine splenocytes isolated from C57BL/6 mice, which preserve endogenous interactions among macrophages, dendritic cells, and lymphocytes, were employed as a systems-level screening matrix. Each PNP preparation from the 110-species library was evaluated under standardized conditions normalized to particle number to ensure comparability across botanically diverse sources ( Fig. 2 A). This design enabled quantitative mapping of functional performance across the nanoparticle library.
Functional screening identifies ANPs as potent immunomodulatory nanomaterials . (A) Schematic illustration of the ex vivo screening assay using murine splenocytes. (B) Screening of 110 PNPs for IFN-γ induction. Top-performing PNPs (highlighted in blue) were selected for further analysis. (C) IL-10 concentrations in splenocyte supernatants following 24 h treatment (ELISA, n = 3). (D) Biocompatibility of PNPs in human PBMCs assessed by flow cytometry (Live/Dead staining) after 48 h incubation. Data are presented as the percentage of live cells ( n = 4). (E) Frequency of IFN-γ + human PBMCs after 24 h treatment ( n = 4). (F) Comparative analysis of IFN-γ induction by ANPs relative to other Allium -derived nanoparticles. AcNPs, Allium cepa -NPs (onion); AfNPs, Allium fistulosum -NPs (spring onion); AsNPs, Allium sativum -NPs (garlic); AwNPs, Allium wakegi Araki -NPs (green onion). Representative flow cytometry histograms (left) and corresponding quantification (right, n = 3). (G) Representative flow cytometry plots (left) and corresponding quantificatio

### 2.3. ANPs drive M1 macrophage-mediated antitumor efficacy in vivo
To evaluate whether the immunostimulatory signature identified during functional screening translates into a therapeutic response in vivo, we next examined the antitumor performance of ANPs in syngeneic tumor models. In the B16F10 melanoma model, intravenous administration of ANPs resulted in a pronounced suppression of tumor growth compared with controls ( Fig. 3 A and B and Fig. S9 ). Immunophenotypic analysis of the tumor microenvironment revealed that ANPs induced a coordinated remodeling of myeloid and lymphoid compartments. Specifically, ANP treatment significantly increased the proportion of CD11b + macrophages expressing M1-associated markers (CD80 and TNF-α) while concomitantly enhancing the infiltration and effector activation of CD8 + T cells ( Fig. 3 C, Fig. S10, and Fig. S11A ). These effects were not restricted to the tumor site but were also evident in secondary lymphoid organs, including the spleen and tumor-draining lymph nodes ( Fig. 3 D, Fig. S11B and Fig. S11C ), indicating that ANPs act as systemically active immunomodulatory nanomaterials rather than locally confined agents. Immunohistochemical analysis further confirmed broad immune cell recruitment, including CD3 ε + T cells, F4/80+ macrophages, and CD11c + antigen-presenting cells, in ANP-treated tumors ( Fig. S12 ). While proliferative activity, assessed by Ki-67 staining, remained unchanged, cleaved caspase-3 levels were significantly elevated, indicating enhanced apoptotic signaling within the tumor tissue.
In vivo antitumor efficacy of ANPs mediated by macrophage reprogramming . (A) Schematic overview of the experimental design of the syngeneic cancer models. Five days after subcutaneous tumor implantation, PBS (control) or ANPs (2 × 10 11 particles) were administered intravenously. (B) Growth curves of B16F10-luc melanoma tumors in C57BL/6 mice (left) and weights of excised tumors from each group (right, n = 5). (C, D) Flow cytometry analysis of T lymphocytes and macrophages in (C) tumo

### 2.4. ANP-RNA triggers M1 macrophage-mediated immunity via the ANP-miR-488/Bcl6 axis
To more effectively resolve bioactive cargo encoded within ANPs, ultracentrifugation-isolated ANPs were further purified by density gradient purification ( Fig. S20 ). Subsequently, functional contributions of distinct components were dissected at the nanomaterial level by reconstituting DNA, RNA, or metabolite fractions extracted from ANPs into liposomes, while lipid shells were applied directly. Protein-associated effects were examined independently using heat-inactivated ANPs ( Fig. S21 ). Notably, in RAW 264.7 cells, treatment with the isolated RNA induced the most pronounced morphological changes ( Fig. 4 A) and a robust upregulation of M1-associated markers (CD80, CD86, MHC-I, iNOS, and TNF-α) along with IFN-γ, as revealed by flow cytometry ( Fig. 4 B and Fig. S22A ). The preserved bioactivity of heat-inactivated ANPs indicates that the observed immunostimulatory function is not protein-mediated but instead primarily driven by non-protein components, excluding proteins as dominant functional mediators. In addition, treatment with isolated RNA cargo induced a clear dose-dependent increase in IFN-γ and CD80 expression ( Fig. S22B and Fig. S23 ).
RNA cargo mediates the immunomodulatory activity of ANPs . (A) Representative microscopy images of RAW 264.7 macrophages treated with PBS (Con), LPS, ANPs, commercial Lipofectamine 3000 alone (Lipo), ANP‐derived lipid shell (Shell), heat‐inactivated ANPs, ANP‐derived RNA-loaded liposomes (RNA), or metabolite‐loaded liposomes (Metabolite). Arrows indicate cells exhibiting activation-associated morphological changes. Scale bars, 100 μm (B) Representative flow cytometry histograms of CD86, MHC‐I, and TNF‐α expression in RAW 264.7 macrophages treated with individual ANP cargo components. (C) Schematic overview of ANP‐RNA generation. The lipid fraction and total RNA were independently isolated from ANPs and subsequently reconstituted into RNA‐reloaded nanoparticles using a standard encapsulation procedure. (D) RT-qPCR analysi

### 2.5. Clinical relevance and synergistic αPD-1 efficacy of ANPs
To explore the clinical relevance of macrophage state regulation associated with the ANP-miR-488/ BCL6 axis, human single-cell and bulk transcriptomes were integrated. A macrophage-enriched signature was defined using lung adenocarcinoma (LUAD) single-cell data ( GSE131907 ) ( Fig. 6 A). This signature was then applied to LUAD and lung squamous cell carcinoma cohorts to stratify macrophage-enriched patient samples, which were further classified into BCL6 high and BCL6 low groups ( Fig. S34 ). The BCL6 low group exhibited a pronounced immune-inflamed phenotype, characterized by elevated expression of M1-associated inflammatory markers ( TNF, CD80, CD86, CXCL9 , and CXCL10 ), whereas immunosuppressive markers ( ARG1, CD163 ) were reduced or unchanged ( Fig. 6 B). Moreover, CD8 + T cell infiltration ( CD8A, CD8B, CXCR3 ) and effector activation signatures ( IFNG, GZMB, PRF1 ) were markedly upregulated in the BCL6 low group ( Fig. 6 C), recapitulating the macrophage-T cell crosstalk observed in our experimental models.
Clinical relevance of ANPs associated with BCL6-dependent immune remodeling and enhanced response to αPD-1 therapy. (A) Identification of a macrophage-enriched gene signature using the GSE131907 lung adenocarcinoma (LUAD) single-cell RNA-sequencing dataset. (B, C) Transcriptomic analysis of macrophage-enriched TCGA lung cancer cohorts (LUAD and LUSC) stratified into BCL6 high and BCL6 low groups. (B) Violin plots showing the expression of M1-associated inflammatory markers ( TNF , CD80 , CD86 , CXCL9 , CXCL10 ) and immunosuppressive markers ( ARG1 , CD163 ). (C) Violin plots showing genes associated with CD8 + T cell infiltration ( CD8A , CD8B , CXCR3 ) and cytotoxic effector molecules ( IFNG , GZMB , PRF1 ). (D) Association between BCL6 expression and clinical response to αPD-1 therapy in the GSE299686 cohort. Macrophage-enriched patients were stratified by BCL6 expression level, and the proportions of responders versus non-responders were compared. (E) 

### 3. Conclusion
In this study, we establish a systematic discovery framework for bioactive plant-derived nanoparticles (PNPs) that moves beyond the conventional one-plant-one-function paradigm. By constructing and physicochemically mapping a taxonomically diverse library of 110 PNPs, we demonstrate that biological functionality can be quantitatively assessed through functional screening, enabling unbiased identification of functional bio-nanomaterials from natural diversity. Using this approach, ANPs were identified as a previously unrecognized functional PNP with pronounced immunomodulatory activity, emerging from systematic screening rather than predefined molecular design.
Mechanistic analyses revealed that ANP bioactivity is primarily associated with RNA cargo carried by the PNPs. In particular, ANP-miR-488 suppresses the transcriptional regulator Bcl6, establishing a clear structure-function relationship that links nanoscale carrier architecture to immune modulation. These findings suggest that RNA functions as a key active component contributing to the immunomodulatory activity of ANPs.

### Ethics approval and consent to participate
All animal procedures were conducted in accordance with protocols approved by the Kyungpook National University Institutional Animal Care and Use Committee (IACUC; approval number: KNU-2025-0783).
PBMCs from healthy donors were obtained from Kyungpook National University Medical Center (KNUMC). Blood collection was approved by the Institutional Review Board of KNUMC (Approval No. 2022-02-011), and written informed consent was obtained from all donors.

PMCID: PMC13316234 PMID: 42381966

### Abstract
Plant-derived nanoparticles (PNPs) have emerged as a promising class of bioactive nanomaterials with intrinsic immunomodulatory properties, offering a sustainable and biocompatible alternative to synthetic delivery systems. However, their systematic exploration has been hindered by a fragmented “one-plant-one-function” research paradigm. Here, we establish a comprehensive bio-discovery platform by constructing a taxonomically diverse library of 110 PNPs. Through integrated physicochemical characterization and high-throughput functional screening, we identify Allium tuberosum -derived nanoparticles (ANPs) as a potent bioactive nanoparticle that effectively promotes pro-inflammatory macrophage polarization. Mechanistically, we demonstrate that the bioactivity of ANPs is largely mediated by their endogenous RNA cargo, specifically ANP-miR-488, which modulates the Bcl6 signaling axis to initiate macrophage reprogramming. Furthermore, ANPs significantly sensitize cold tumors to immune checkpoint blockade therapy in a syngeneic LLC1 lung carcinoma model. This study not only provides a generalizable strategy for the systematic discovery of bioactive nanomaterials but also highlights the clinical potential of RNA-mediated PNPs as a promising platform for enhancing cancer immunotherapy.

### Highlights
• Plant nanoparticle library enables unbiased discovery of bioactive nanomaterials.
• Allium tuberosum nanoparticles induce M1 macrophages to enhance tumor immunity.
• miR-488 of Allium tuberosum nanoparticles reprograms macrophages via Bcl6 regulation.
• Allium tuberosum nanoparticles remodel tumor microenvironment for anti-PD-1 therapy.

### 1. Introduction
Nanomaterials with defined nanoscale architectures have enabled diverse applications in drug delivery, imaging, and immunotherapy [ [1] , [2] , [3] , [4] ]. In synthetic systems, rigorous control over chemical composition, structural geometry, and surface functionalization has enabled the engineering of specific functional properties into nanoscale carriers [ [5] , [6] , [7] ]. However, these design strategies have been largely restricted to engineered synthetic nanomaterials, while naturally occurring nanoscale systems have rarely been systematically explored as bioactive nanomaterial platforms [ 8 , 9 ]. Nature-derived nanomaterials offer a sophisticated alternative where nanoscale organization is shaped by biological evolution, providing structurally complex and highly biocompatible systems with inherent functional potential [ 10 , 11 ].
Among these, plant-derived nanoparticles (PNPs) have recently emerged as a promising class of bioactive nanomaterials, characterized by high biocompatibility, low toxicity, and a diverse repertoire of molecular cargo [ 8 , [12] , [13] , [14] ]. These PNPs are enriched with endogenous lipids, proteins, and nucleic acids and can be sustainably produced at high yields from renewable botanical sources [ 15 , 16 ]. Emerging evidence suggests that PNPs function not only as inert delivery vehicles but also active modulators capable of modulating complex biological processes, including targeted drug delivery [ [17] , [18] , [19] ], inflammation regulation [ [20] , [21] , [22] , [23] ], and cancer therapy [ [24] , [25] , [26] , [27] ].
Despite these rapid advances, current investigations into PNPs remain largely phenomenological and fragmented [ 28 , 29 ]. Most studies focus on a single plant species selected based on empirical or ethnopharmacological knowledge, following a restrictive “one-plant-one-function” paradigm reminiscent of early natural product research [ 30 ]. This conventional approach limits systematic comparison across dive

### 2.1. Library construction and physicochemical mapping of PNPs
To enable systematic discovery of bioactive nanomaterials, we constructed a comprehensive library of PNPs encompassing 110 botanically diverse plant species across 34 taxonomic families ( Fig. 1 A and Table S1 ). Unlike conventional studies focusing on individual plants, we deliberately designed this comprehensive library as a comparative nanomaterial platform to systematically investigate complex structure-function relationships across a diverse range of species. PNPs were isolated from multiple plant tissues, including leaves, aerial parts, fruits, and roots, thereby capturing intrinsic variability in nanoscale architecture and molecular organization ( Fig. S1 ). To ensure that functional differences arise from intrinsic nanoparticle properties rather than isolation artifacts, we implemented a standardized, low-shear extraction and differential centrifugation workflow ( Fig. 1 B). This protocol enabled reproducible fabrication of nanoscale carriers across all plant sources, independent of tissue texture or water content.
Construction and physicochemical characterization of the PNP library . (A) Taxonomic analysis of the 110 plant species comprising the PNP library. The library includes 59 edible plants and 51 medicinal plants, spanning 34 families, with prominent representation from Asteraceae, Brassicaceae, and Fabaceae. Within the library, certain species include distinct cultivars (e.g., Capsicum annuum varieties) or specific plant tissues (e.g., Senna tora seed, stem, leaf). These entries are indicated as extended segments in the outer ring. (B) Schematic overview of the PNP isolation protocol applied uniformly across 110 plant species. The process consists of low-speed cold-press extraction to preserve nanoparticle integrity, followed by purification via differential centrifugation. All centrifugation steps were conducted at 4 °C. (C) Representative transmission electron microscopy (TEM) images of eight PNP species, randomly selected across plant tissue categ

### 2.2. Functional screening as materials discovery
To enable systematic discovery of functional PNPs as active bio-nanomaterials, we employ interferon-γ (IFN-γ) production as a quantitative functional screening readout [ 36 , 37 ] . IFN-γ represents an emergent, integrative output of multicellular immune crosstalk and thus serves as a sensitive proxy for identifying nanomaterials capable of modulating complex biological responses, rather than merely interacting with isolated cell types. Primary murine splenocytes isolated from C57BL/6 mice, which preserve endogenous interactions among macrophages, dendritic cells, and lymphocytes, were employed as a systems-level screening matrix. Each PNP preparation from the 110-species library was evaluated under standardized conditions normalized to particle number to ensure comparability across botanically diverse sources ( Fig. 2 A). This design enabled quantitative mapping of functional performance across the nanoparticle library.
Functional screening identifies ANPs as potent immunomodulatory nanomaterials . (A) Schematic illustration of the ex vivo screening assay using murine splenocytes. (B) Screening of 110 PNPs for IFN-γ induction. Top-performing PNPs (highlighted in blue) were selected for further analysis. (C) IL-10 concentrations in splenocyte supernatants following 24 h treatment (ELISA, n = 3). (D) Biocompatibility of PNPs in human PBMCs assessed by flow cytometry (Live/Dead staining) after 48 h incubation. Data are presented as the percentage of live cells ( n = 4). (E) Frequency of IFN-γ + human PBMCs after 24 h treatment ( n = 4). (F) Comparative analysis of IFN-γ induction by ANPs relative to other Allium -derived nanoparticles. AcNPs, Allium cepa -NPs (onion); AfNPs, Allium fistulosum -NPs (spring onion); AsNPs, Allium sativum -NPs (garlic); AwNPs, Allium wakegi Araki -NPs (green onion). Representative flow cytometry histograms (left) and corresponding quantification (right, n = 3). (G) Representative flow cytometry plots (left) and corresponding quantificatio

### 2.3. ANPs drive M1 macrophage-mediated antitumor efficacy in vivo
To evaluate whether the immunostimulatory signature identified during functional screening translates into a therapeutic response in vivo, we next examined the antitumor performance of ANPs in syngeneic tumor models. In the B16F10 melanoma model, intravenous administration of ANPs resulted in a pronounced suppression of tumor growth compared with controls ( Fig. 3 A and B and Fig. S9 ). Immunophenotypic analysis of the tumor microenvironment revealed that ANPs induced a coordinated remodeling of myeloid and lymphoid compartments. Specifically, ANP treatment significantly increased the proportion of CD11b + macrophages expressing M1-associated markers (CD80 and TNF-α) while concomitantly enhancing the infiltration and effector activation of CD8 + T cells ( Fig. 3 C, Fig. S10, and Fig. S11A ). These effects were not restricted to the tumor site but were also evident in secondary lymphoid organs, including the spleen and tumor-draining lymph nodes ( Fig. 3 D, Fig. S11B and Fig. S11C ), indicating that ANPs act as systemically active immunomodulatory nanomaterials rather than locally confined agents. Immunohistochemical analysis further confirmed broad immune cell recruitment, including CD3 ε + T cells, F4/80+ macrophages, and CD11c + antigen-presenting cells, in ANP-treated tumors ( Fig. S12 ). While proliferative activity, assessed by Ki-67 staining, remained unchanged, cleaved caspase-3 levels were significantly elevated, indicating enhanced apoptotic signaling within the tumor tissue.
In vivo antitumor efficacy of ANPs mediated by macrophage reprogramming . (A) Schematic overview of the experimental design of the syngeneic cancer models. Five days after subcutaneous tumor implantation, PBS (control) or ANPs (2 × 10 11 particles) were administered intravenously. (B) Growth curves of B16F10-luc melanoma tumors in C57BL/6 mice (left) and weights of excised tumors from each group (right, n = 5). (C, D) Flow cytometry analysis of T lymphocytes and macrophages in (C) tumo

### 2.4. ANP-RNA triggers M1 macrophage-mediated immunity via the ANP-miR-488/Bcl6 axis
To more effectively resolve bioactive cargo encoded within ANPs, ultracentrifugation-isolated ANPs were further purified by density gradient purification ( Fig. S20 ). Subsequently, functional contributions of distinct components were dissected at the nanomaterial level by reconstituting DNA, RNA, or metabolite fractions extracted from ANPs into liposomes, while lipid shells were applied directly. Protein-associated effects were examined independently using heat-inactivated ANPs ( Fig. S21 ). Notably, in RAW 264.7 cells, treatment with the isolated RNA induced the most pronounced morphological changes ( Fig. 4 A) and a robust upregulation of M1-associated markers (CD80, CD86, MHC-I, iNOS, and TNF-α) along with IFN-γ, as revealed by flow cytometry ( Fig. 4 B and Fig. S22A ). The preserved bioactivity of heat-inactivated ANPs indicates that the observed immunostimulatory function is not protein-mediated but instead primarily driven by non-protein components, excluding proteins as dominant functional mediators. In addition, treatment with isolated RNA cargo induced a clear dose-dependent increase in IFN-γ and CD80 expression ( Fig. S22B and Fig. S23 ).
RNA cargo mediates the immunomodulatory activity of ANPs . (A) Representative microscopy images of RAW 264.7 macrophages treated with PBS (Con), LPS, ANPs, commercial Lipofectamine 3000 alone (Lipo), ANP‐derived lipid shell (Shell), heat‐inactivated ANPs, ANP‐derived RNA-loaded liposomes (RNA), or metabolite‐loaded liposomes (Metabolite). Arrows indicate cells exhibiting activation-associated morphological changes. Scale bars, 100 μm (B) Representative flow cytometry histograms of CD86, MHC‐I, and TNF‐α expression in RAW 264.7 macrophages treated with individual ANP cargo components. (C) Schematic overview of ANP‐RNA generation. The lipid fraction and total RNA were independently isolated from ANPs and subsequently reconstituted into RNA‐reloaded nanoparticles using a standard encapsulation procedure. (D) RT-qPCR analysi

### 2.5. Clinical relevance and synergistic αPD-1 efficacy of ANPs
To explore the clinical relevance of macrophage state regulation associated with the ANP-miR-488/ BCL6 axis, human single-cell and bulk transcriptomes were integrated. A macrophage-enriched signature was defined using lung adenocarcinoma (LUAD) single-cell data ( GSE131907 ) ( Fig. 6 A). This signature was then applied to LUAD and lung squamous cell carcinoma cohorts to stratify macrophage-enriched patient samples, which were further classified into BCL6 high and BCL6 low groups ( Fig. S34 ). The BCL6 low group exhibited a pronounced immune-inflamed phenotype, characterized by elevated expression of M1-associated inflammatory markers ( TNF, CD80, CD86, CXCL9 , and CXCL10 ), whereas immunosuppressive markers ( ARG1, CD163 ) were reduced or unchanged ( Fig. 6 B). Moreover, CD8 + T cell infiltration ( CD8A, CD8B, CXCR3 ) and effector activation signatures ( IFNG, GZMB, PRF1 ) were markedly upregulated in the BCL6 low group ( Fig. 6 C), recapitulating the macrophage-T cell crosstalk observed in our experimental models.
Clinical relevance of ANPs associated with BCL6-dependent immune remodeling and enhanced response to αPD-1 therapy. (A) Identification of a macrophage-enriched gene signature using the GSE131907 lung adenocarcinoma (LUAD) single-cell RNA-sequencing dataset. (B, C) Transcriptomic analysis of macrophage-enriched TCGA lung cancer cohorts (LUAD and LUSC) stratified into BCL6 high and BCL6 low groups. (B) Violin plots showing the expression of M1-associated inflammatory markers ( TNF , CD80 , CD86 , CXCL9 , CXCL10 ) and immunosuppressive markers ( ARG1 , CD163 ). (C) Violin plots showing genes associated with CD8 + T cell infiltration ( CD8A , CD8B , CXCR3 ) and cytotoxic effector molecules ( IFNG , GZMB , PRF1 ). (D) Association between BCL6 expression and clinical response to αPD-1 therapy in the GSE299686 cohort. Macrophage-enriched patients were stratified by BCL6 expression level, and the proportions of responders versus non-responders were compared. (E) 

### 3. Conclusion
In this study, we establish a systematic discovery framework for bioactive plant-derived nanoparticles (PNPs) that moves beyond the conventional one-plant-one-function paradigm. By constructing and physicochemically mapping a taxonomically diverse library of 110 PNPs, we demonstrate that biological functionality can be quantitatively assessed through functional screening, enabling unbiased identification of functional bio-nanomaterials from natural diversity. Using this approach, ANPs were identified as a previously unrecognized functional PNP with pronounced immunomodulatory activity, emerging from systematic screening rather than predefined molecular design.
Mechanistic analyses revealed that ANP bioactivity is primarily associated with RNA cargo carried by the PNPs. In particular, ANP-miR-488 suppresses the transcriptional regulator Bcl6, establishing a clear structure-function relationship that links nanoscale carrier architecture to immune modulation. These findings suggest that RNA functions as a key active component contributing to the immunomodulatory activity of ANPs.

### Ethics approval and consent to participate
All animal procedures were conducted in accordance with protocols approved by the Kyungpook National University Institutional Animal Care and Use Committee (IACUC; approval number: KNU-2025-0783).
PBMCs from healthy donors were obtained from Kyungpook National University Medical Center (KNUMC). Blood collection was approved by the Institutional Review Board of KNUMC (Approval No. 2022-02-011), and written informed consent was obtained from all donors.

### Abstract
Plant-derived nanoparticles (PNPs) have emerged as a promising class of bioactive nanomaterials with intrinsic immunomodulatory properties, offering a sustainable and biocompatible alternative to synthetic delivery systems. However, their systematic exploration has been hindered by a fragmented “one-plant-one-function” research paradigm. Here, we establish a comprehensive bio-discovery platform by constructing a taxonomically diverse library of 110 PNPs. Through integrated physicochemical characterization and high-throughput functional screening, we identify Allium tuberosum -derived nanoparticles (ANPs) as a potent bioactive nanoparticle that effectively promotes pro-inflammatory macrophage polarization. Mechanistically, we demonstrate that the bioactivity of ANPs is largely mediated by their endogenous RNA cargo, specifically ANP-miR-488, which modulates the Bcl6 signaling axis to initiate macrophage reprogramming. Furthermore, ANPs significantly sensitize cold tumors to immune checkpoint blockade therapy in a syngeneic LLC1 lung carcinoma model. This study not only provides a generalizable strategy for the systematic discovery of bioactive nanomaterials but also highlights the clinical potential of RNA-mediated PNPs as a promising platform for enhancing cancer immunotherapy.

### Highlights
• Plant nanoparticle library enables unbiased discovery of bioactive nanomaterials.
• Allium tuberosum nanoparticles induce M1 macrophages to enhance tumor immunity.
• miR-488 of Allium tuberosum nanoparticles reprograms macrophages via Bcl6 regulation.
• Allium tuberosum nanoparticles remodel tumor microenvironment for anti-PD-1 therapy.

### 1. Introduction
Nanomaterials with defined nanoscale architectures have enabled diverse applications in drug delivery, imaging, and immunotherapy [ [1] , [2] , [3] , [4] ]. In synthetic systems, rigorous control over chemical composition, structural geometry, and surface functionalization has enabled the engineering of specific functional properties into nanoscale carriers [ [5] , [6] , [7] ]. However, these design strategies have been largely restricted to engineered synthetic nanomaterials, while naturally occurring nanoscale systems have rarely been systematically explored as bioactive nanomaterial platforms [ 8 , 9 ]. Nature-derived nanomaterials offer a sophisticated alternative where nanoscale organization is shaped by biological evolution, providing structurally complex and highly biocompatible systems with inherent functional potential [ 10 , 11 ].
Among these, plant-derived nanoparticles (PNPs) have recently emerged as a promising class of bioactive nanomaterials, characterized by high biocompatibility, low toxicity, and a diverse repertoire of molecular cargo [ 8 , [12] , [13] , [14] ]. These PNPs are enriched with endogenous lipids, proteins, and nucleic acids and can be sustainably produced at high yields from renewable botanical sources [ 15 , 16 ]. Emerging evidence suggests that PNPs function not only as inert delivery vehicles but also active modulators capable of modulating complex biological processes, including targeted drug delivery [ [17] , [18] , [19] ], inflammation regulation [ [20] , [21] , [22] , [23] ], and cancer therapy [ [24] , [25] , [26] , [27] ].
Despite these rapid advances, current investigations into PNPs remain largely phenomenological and fragmented [ 28 , 29 ]. Most studies focus on a single plant species selected based on empirical or ethnopharmacological knowledge, following a restrictive “one-plant-one-function” paradigm reminiscent of early natural product research [ 30 ]. This conventional approach limits systematic comparison across dive

### 2.1. Library construction and physicochemical mapping of PNPs
To enable systematic discovery of bioactive nanomaterials, we constructed a comprehensive library of PNPs encompassing 110 botanically diverse plant species across 34 taxonomic families ( Fig. 1 A and Table S1 ). Unlike conventional studies focusing on individual plants, we deliberately designed this comprehensive library as a comparative nanomaterial platform to systematically investigate complex structure-function relationships across a diverse range of species. PNPs were isolated from multiple plant tissues, including leaves, aerial parts, fruits, and roots, thereby capturing intrinsic variability in nanoscale architecture and molecular organization ( Fig. S1 ). To ensure that functional differences arise from intrinsic nanoparticle properties rather than isolation artifacts, we implemented a standardized, low-shear extraction and differential centrifugation workflow ( Fig. 1 B). This protocol enabled reproducible fabrication of nanoscale carriers across all plant sources, independent of tissue texture or water content.
Construction and physicochemical characterization of the PNP library . (A) Taxonomic analysis of the 110 plant species comprising the PNP library. The library includes 59 edible plants and 51 medicinal plants, spanning 34 families, with prominent representation from Asteraceae, Brassicaceae, and Fabaceae. Within the library, certain species include distinct cultivars (e.g., Capsicum annuum varieties) or specific plant tissues (e.g., Senna tora seed, stem, leaf). These entries are indicated as extended segments in the outer ring. (B) Schematic overview of the PNP isolation protocol applied uniformly across 110 plant species. The process consists of low-speed cold-press extraction to preserve nanoparticle integrity, followed by purification via differential centrifugation. All centrifugation steps were conducted at 4 °C. (C) Representative transmission electron microscopy (TEM) images of eight PNP species, randomly selected across plant tissue categ

### 2.2. Functional screening as materials discovery
To enable systematic discovery of functional PNPs as active bio-nanomaterials, we employ interferon-γ (IFN-γ) production as a quantitative functional screening readout [ 36 , 37 ] . IFN-γ represents an emergent, integrative output of multicellular immune crosstalk and thus serves as a sensitive proxy for identifying nanomaterials capable of modulating complex biological responses, rather than merely interacting with isolated cell types. Primary murine splenocytes isolated from C57BL/6 mice, which preserve endogenous interactions among macrophages, dendritic cells, and lymphocytes, were employed as a systems-level screening matrix. Each PNP preparation from the 110-species library was evaluated under standardized conditions normalized to particle number to ensure comparability across botanically diverse sources ( Fig. 2 A). This design enabled quantitative mapping of functional performance across the nanoparticle library.
Functional screening identifies ANPs as potent immunomodulatory nanomaterials . (A) Schematic illustration of the ex vivo screening assay using murine splenocytes. (B) Screening of 110 PNPs for IFN-γ induction. Top-performing PNPs (highlighted in blue) were selected for further analysis. (C) IL-10 concentrations in splenocyte supernatants following 24 h treatment (ELISA, n = 3). (D) Biocompatibility of PNPs in human PBMCs assessed by flow cytometry (Live/Dead staining) after 48 h incubation. Data are presented as the percentage of live cells ( n = 4). (E) Frequency of IFN-γ + human PBMCs after 24 h treatment ( n = 4). (F) Comparative analysis of IFN-γ induction by ANPs relative to other Allium -derived nanoparticles. AcNPs, Allium cepa -NPs (onion); AfNPs, Allium fistulosum -NPs (spring onion); AsNPs, Allium sativum -NPs (garlic); AwNPs, Allium wakegi Araki -NPs (green onion). Representative flow cytometry histograms (left) and corresponding quantification (right, n = 3). (G) Representative flow cytometry plots (left) and corresponding quantificatio

### 2.3. ANPs drive M1 macrophage-mediated antitumor efficacy in vivo
To evaluate whether the immunostimulatory signature identified during functional screening translates into a therapeutic response in vivo, we next examined the antitumor performance of ANPs in syngeneic tumor models. In the B16F10 melanoma model, intravenous administration of ANPs resulted in a pronounced suppression of tumor growth compared with controls ( Fig. 3 A and B and Fig. S9 ). Immunophenotypic analysis of the tumor microenvironment revealed that ANPs induced a coordinated remodeling of myeloid and lymphoid compartments. Specifically, ANP treatment significantly increased the proportion of CD11b + macrophages expressing M1-associated markers (CD80 and TNF-α) while concomitantly enhancing the infiltration and effector activation of CD8 + T cells ( Fig. 3 C, Fig. S10, and Fig. S11A ). These effects were not restricted to the tumor site but were also evident in secondary lymphoid organs, including the spleen and tumor-draining lymph nodes ( Fig. 3 D, Fig. S11B and Fig. S11C ), indicating that ANPs act as systemically active immunomodulatory nanomaterials rather than locally confined agents. Immunohistochemical analysis further confirmed broad immune cell recruitment, including CD3 ε + T cells, F4/80+ macrophages, and CD11c + antigen-presenting cells, in ANP-treated tumors ( Fig. S12 ). While proliferative activity, assessed by Ki-67 staining, remained unchanged, cleaved caspase-3 levels were significantly elevated, indicating enhanced apoptotic signaling within the tumor tissue.
In vivo antitumor efficacy of ANPs mediated by macrophage reprogramming . (A) Schematic overview of the experimental design of the syngeneic cancer models. Five days after subcutaneous tumor implantation, PBS (control) or ANPs (2 × 10 11 particles) were administered intravenously. (B) Growth curves of B16F10-luc melanoma tumors in C57BL/6 mice (left) and weights of excised tumors from each group (right, n = 5). (C, D) Flow cytometry analysis of T lymphocytes and macrophages in (C) tumo

### 2.4. ANP-RNA triggers M1 macrophage-mediated immunity via the ANP-miR-488/Bcl6 axis
To more effectively resolve bioactive cargo encoded within ANPs, ultracentrifugation-isolated ANPs were further purified by density gradient purification ( Fig. S20 ). Subsequently, functional contributions of distinct components were dissected at the nanomaterial level by reconstituting DNA, RNA, or metabolite fractions extracted from ANPs into liposomes, while lipid shells were applied directly. Protein-associated effects were examined independently using heat-inactivated ANPs ( Fig. S21 ). Notably, in RAW 264.7 cells, treatment with the isolated RNA induced the most pronounced morphological changes ( Fig. 4 A) and a robust upregulation of M1-associated markers (CD80, CD86, MHC-I, iNOS, and TNF-α) along with IFN-γ, as revealed by flow cytometry ( Fig. 4 B and Fig. S22A ). The preserved bioactivity of heat-inactivated ANPs indicates that the observed immunostimulatory function is not protein-mediated but instead primarily driven by non-protein components, excluding proteins as dominant functional mediators. In addition, treatment with isolated RNA cargo induced a clear dose-dependent increase in IFN-γ and CD80 expression ( Fig. S22B and Fig. S23 ).
RNA cargo mediates the immunomodulatory activity of ANPs . (A) Representative microscopy images of RAW 264.7 macrophages treated with PBS (Con), LPS, ANPs, commercial Lipofectamine 3000 alone (Lipo), ANP‐derived lipid shell (Shell), heat‐inactivated ANPs, ANP‐derived RNA-loaded liposomes (RNA), or metabolite‐loaded liposomes (Metabolite). Arrows indicate cells exhibiting activation-associated morphological changes. Scale bars, 100 μm (B) Representative flow cytometry histograms of CD86, MHC‐I, and TNF‐α expression in RAW 264.7 macrophages treated with individual ANP cargo components. (C) Schematic overview of ANP‐RNA generation. The lipid fraction and total RNA were independently isolated from ANPs and subsequently reconstituted into RNA‐reloaded nanoparticles using a standard encapsulation procedure. (D) RT-qPCR analysi

### 2.5. Clinical relevance and synergistic αPD-1 efficacy of ANPs
To explore the clinical relevance of macrophage state regulation associated with the ANP-miR-488/ BCL6 axis, human single-cell and bulk transcriptomes were integrated. A macrophage-enriched signature was defined using lung adenocarcinoma (LUAD) single-cell data ( GSE131907 ) ( Fig. 6 A). This signature was then applied to LUAD and lung squamous cell carcinoma cohorts to stratify macrophage-enriched patient samples, which were further classified into BCL6 high and BCL6 low groups ( Fig. S34 ). The BCL6 low group exhibited a pronounced immune-inflamed phenotype, characterized by elevated expression of M1-associated inflammatory markers ( TNF, CD80, CD86, CXCL9 , and CXCL10 ), whereas immunosuppressive markers ( ARG1, CD163 ) were reduced or unchanged ( Fig. 6 B). Moreover, CD8 + T cell infiltration ( CD8A, CD8B, CXCR3 ) and effector activation signatures ( IFNG, GZMB, PRF1 ) were markedly upregulated in the BCL6 low group ( Fig. 6 C), recapitulating the macrophage-T cell crosstalk observed in our experimental models.
Clinical relevance of ANPs associated with BCL6-dependent immune remodeling and enhanced response to αPD-1 therapy. (A) Identification of a macrophage-enriched gene signature using the GSE131907 lung adenocarcinoma (LUAD) single-cell RNA-sequencing dataset. (B, C) Transcriptomic analysis of macrophage-enriched TCGA lung cancer cohorts (LUAD and LUSC) stratified into BCL6 high and BCL6 low groups. (B) Violin plots showing the expression of M1-associated inflammatory markers ( TNF , CD80 , CD86 , CXCL9 , CXCL10 ) and immunosuppressive markers ( ARG1 , CD163 ). (C) Violin plots showing genes associated with CD8 + T cell infiltration ( CD8A , CD8B , CXCR3 ) and cytotoxic effector molecules ( IFNG , GZMB , PRF1 ). (D) Association between BCL6 expression and clinical response to αPD-1 therapy in the GSE299686 cohort. Macrophage-enriched patients were stratified by BCL6 expression level, and the proportions of responders versus non-responders were compared. (E) 

### 3. Conclusion
In this study, we establish a systematic discovery framework for bioactive plant-derived nanoparticles (PNPs) that moves beyond the conventional one-plant-one-function paradigm. By constructing and physicochemically mapping a taxonomically diverse library of 110 PNPs, we demonstrate that biological functionality can be quantitatively assessed through functional screening, enabling unbiased identification of functional bio-nanomaterials from natural diversity. Using this approach, ANPs were identified as a previously unrecognized functional PNP with pronounced immunomodulatory activity, emerging from systematic screening rather than predefined molecular design.
Mechanistic analyses revealed that ANP bioactivity is primarily associated with RNA cargo carried by the PNPs. In particular, ANP-miR-488 suppresses the transcriptional regulator Bcl6, establishing a clear structure-function relationship that links nanoscale carrier architecture to immune modulation. These findings suggest that RNA functions as a key active component contributing to the immunomodulatory activity of ANPs.

### Ethics approval and consent to participate
All animal procedures were conducted in accordance with protocols approved by the Kyungpook National University Institutional Animal Care and Use Committee (IACUC; approval number: KNU-2025-0783).
PBMCs from healthy donors were obtained from Kyungpook National University Medical Center (KNUMC). Blood collection was approved by the Institutional Review Board of KNUMC (Approval No. 2022-02-011), and written informed consent was obtained from all donors.

### Abstract
Plant-derived nanoparticles (PNPs) have emerged as a promising class of bioactive nanomaterials with intrinsic immunomodulatory properties, offering a sustainable and biocompatible alternative to synthetic delivery systems. However, their systematic exploration has been hindered by a fragmented “one-plant-one-function” research paradigm. Here, we establish a comprehensive bio-discovery platform by constructing a taxonomically diverse library of 110 PNPs. Through integrated physicochemical characterization and high-throughput functional screening, we identify Allium tuberosum -derived nanoparticles (ANPs) as a potent bioactive nanoparticle that effectively promotes pro-inflammatory macrophage polarization. Mechanistically, we demonstrate that the bioactivity of ANPs is largely mediated by their endogenous RNA cargo, specifically ANP-miR-488, which modulates the Bcl6 signaling axis to initiate macrophage reprogramming. Furthermore, ANPs significantly sensitize cold tumors to immune checkpoint blockade therapy in a syngeneic LLC1 lung carcinoma model. This study not only provides a generalizable strategy for the systematic discovery of bioactive nanomaterials but also highlights the clinical potential of RNA-mediated PNPs as a promising platform for enhancing cancer immunotherapy.

### Highlights
• Plant nanoparticle library enables unbiased discovery of bioactive nanomaterials.
• Allium tuberosum nanoparticles induce M1 macrophages to enhance tumor immunity.
• miR-488 of Allium tuberosum nanoparticles reprograms macrophages via Bcl6 regulation.
• Allium tuberosum nanoparticles remodel tumor microenvironment for anti-PD-1 therapy.

### 1. Introduction
Nanomaterials with defined nanoscale architectures have enabled diverse applications in drug delivery, imaging, and immunotherapy [ [1] , [2] , [3] , [4] ]. In synthetic systems, rigorous control over chemical composition, structural geometry, and surface functionalization has enabled the engineering of specific functional properties into nanoscale carriers [ [5] , [6] , [7] ]. However, these design strategies have been largely restricted to engineered synthetic nanomaterials, while naturally occurring nanoscale systems have rarely been systematically explored as bioactive nanomaterial platforms [ 8 , 9 ]. Nature-derived nanomaterials offer a sophisticated alternative where nanoscale organization is shaped by biological evolution, providing structurally complex and highly biocompatible systems with inherent functional potential [ 10 , 11 ].
Among these, plant-derived nanoparticles (PNPs) have recently emerged as a promising class of bioactive nanomaterials, characterized by high biocompatibility, low toxicity, and a diverse repertoire of molecular cargo [ 8 , [12] , [13] , [14] ]. These PNPs are enriched with endogenous lipids, proteins, and nucleic acids and can be sustainably produced at high yields from renewable botanical sources [ 15 , 16 ]. Emerging evidence suggests that PNPs function not only as inert delivery vehicles but also active modulators capable of modulating complex biological processes, including targeted drug delivery [ [17] , [18] , [19] ], inflammation regulation [ [20] , [21] , [22] , [23] ], and cancer therapy [ [24] , [25] , [26] , [27] ].
Despite these rapid advances, current investigations into PNPs remain largely phenomenological and fragmented [ 28 , 29 ]. Most studies focus on a single plant species selected based on empirical or ethnopharmacological knowledge, following a restrictive “one-plant-one-function” paradigm reminiscent of early natural product research [ 30 ]. This conventional approach limits systematic comparison across dive

### 2.1. Library construction and physicochemical mapping of PNPs
To enable systematic discovery of bioactive nanomaterials, we constructed a comprehensive library of PNPs encompassing 110 botanically diverse plant species across 34 taxonomic families ( Fig. 1 A and Table S1 ). Unlike conventional studies focusing on individual plants, we deliberately designed this comprehensive library as a comparative nanomaterial platform to systematically investigate complex structure-function relationships across a diverse range of species. PNPs were isolated from multiple plant tissues, including leaves, aerial parts, fruits, and roots, thereby capturing intrinsic variability in nanoscale architecture and molecular organization ( Fig. S1 ). To ensure that functional differences arise from intrinsic nanoparticle properties rather than isolation artifacts, we implemented a standardized, low-shear extraction and differential centrifugation workflow ( Fig. 1 B). This protocol enabled reproducible fabrication of nanoscale carriers across all plant sources, independent of tissue texture or water content.
Construction and physicochemical characterization of the PNP library . (A) Taxonomic analysis of the 110 plant species comprising the PNP library. The library includes 59 edible plants and 51 medicinal plants, spanning 34 families, with prominent representation from Asteraceae, Brassicaceae, and Fabaceae. Within the library, certain species include distinct cultivars (e.g., Capsicum annuum varieties) or specific plant tissues (e.g., Senna tora seed, stem, leaf). These entries are indicated as extended segments in the outer ring. (B) Schematic overview of the PNP isolation protocol applied uniformly across 110 plant species. The process consists of low-speed cold-press extraction to preserve nanoparticle integrity, followed by purification via differential centrifugation. All centrifugation steps were conducted at 4 °C. (C) Representative transmission electron microscopy (TEM) images of eight PNP species, randomly selected across plant tissue categ

### 2.2. Functional screening as materials discovery
To enable systematic discovery of functional PNPs as active bio-nanomaterials, we employ interferon-γ (IFN-γ) production as a quantitative functional screening readout [ 36 , 37 ] . IFN-γ represents an emergent, integrative output of multicellular immune crosstalk and thus serves as a sensitive proxy for identifying nanomaterials capable of modulating complex biological responses, rather than merely interacting with isolated cell types. Primary murine splenocytes isolated from C57BL/6 mice, which preserve endogenous interactions among macrophages, dendritic cells, and lymphocytes, were employed as a systems-level screening matrix. Each PNP preparation from the 110-species library was evaluated under standardized conditions normalized to particle number to ensure comparability across botanically diverse sources ( Fig. 2 A). This design enabled quantitative mapping of functional performance across the nanoparticle library.
Functional screening identifies ANPs as potent immunomodulatory nanomaterials . (A) Schematic illustration of the ex vivo screening assay using murine splenocytes. (B) Screening of 110 PNPs for IFN-γ induction. Top-performing PNPs (highlighted in blue) were selected for further analysis. (C) IL-10 concentrations in splenocyte supernatants following 24 h treatment (ELISA, n = 3). (D) Biocompatibility of PNPs in human PBMCs assessed by flow cytometry (Live/Dead staining) after 48 h incubation. Data are presented as the percentage of live cells ( n = 4). (E) Frequency of IFN-γ + human PBMCs after 24 h treatment ( n = 4). (F) Comparative analysis of IFN-γ induction by ANPs relative to other Allium -derived nanoparticles. AcNPs, Allium cepa -NPs (onion); AfNPs, Allium fistulosum -NPs (spring onion); AsNPs, Allium sativum -NPs (garlic); AwNPs, Allium wakegi Araki -NPs (green onion). Representative flow cytometry histograms (left) and corresponding quantification (right, n = 3). (G) Representative flow cytometry plots (left) and corresponding quantificatio

### 2.3. ANPs drive M1 macrophage-mediated antitumor efficacy in vivo
To evaluate whether the immunostimulatory signature identified during functional screening translates into a therapeutic response in vivo, we next examined the antitumor performance of ANPs in syngeneic tumor models. In the B16F10 melanoma model, intravenous administration of ANPs resulted in a pronounced suppression of tumor growth compared with controls ( Fig. 3 A and B and Fig. S9 ). Immunophenotypic analysis of the tumor microenvironment revealed that ANPs induced a coordinated remodeling of myeloid and lymphoid compartments. Specifically, ANP treatment significantly increased the proportion of CD11b + macrophages expressing M1-associated markers (CD80 and TNF-α) while concomitantly enhancing the infiltration and effector activation of CD8 + T cells ( Fig. 3 C, Fig. S10, and Fig. S11A ). These effects were not restricted to the tumor site but were also evident in secondary lymphoid organs, including the spleen and tumor-draining lymph nodes ( Fig. 3 D, Fig. S11B and Fig. S11C ), indicating that ANPs act as systemically active immunomodulatory nanomaterials rather than locally confined agents. Immunohistochemical analysis further confirmed broad immune cell recruitment, including CD3 ε + T cells, F4/80+ macrophages, and CD11c + antigen-presenting cells, in ANP-treated tumors ( Fig. S12 ). While proliferative activity, assessed by Ki-67 staining, remained unchanged, cleaved caspase-3 levels were significantly elevated, indicating enhanced apoptotic signaling within the tumor tissue.
In vivo antitumor efficacy of ANPs mediated by macrophage reprogramming . (A) Schematic overview of the experimental design of the syngeneic cancer models. Five days after subcutaneous tumor implantation, PBS (control) or ANPs (2 × 10 11 particles) were administered intravenously. (B) Growth curves of B16F10-luc melanoma tumors in C57BL/6 mice (left) and weights of excised tumors from each group (right, n = 5). (C, D) Flow cytometry analysis of T lymphocytes and macrophages in (C) tumo

### 2.4. ANP-RNA triggers M1 macrophage-mediated immunity via the ANP-miR-488/Bcl6 axis
To more effectively resolve bioactive cargo encoded within ANPs, ultracentrifugation-isolated ANPs were further purified by density gradient purification ( Fig. S20 ). Subsequently, functional contributions of distinct components were dissected at the nanomaterial level by reconstituting DNA, RNA, or metabolite fractions extracted from ANPs into liposomes, while lipid shells were applied directly. Protein-associated effects were examined independently using heat-inactivated ANPs ( Fig. S21 ). Notably, in RAW 264.7 cells, treatment with the isolated RNA induced the most pronounced morphological changes ( Fig. 4 A) and a robust upregulation of M1-associated markers (CD80, CD86, MHC-I, iNOS, and TNF-α) along with IFN-γ, as revealed by flow cytometry ( Fig. 4 B and Fig. S22A ). The preserved bioactivity of heat-inactivated ANPs indicates that the observed immunostimulatory function is not protein-mediated but instead primarily driven by non-protein components, excluding proteins as dominant functional mediators. In addition, treatment with isolated RNA cargo induced a clear dose-dependent increase in IFN-γ and CD80 expression ( Fig. S22B and Fig. S23 ).
RNA cargo mediates the immunomodulatory activity of ANPs . (A) Representative microscopy images of RAW 264.7 macrophages treated with PBS (Con), LPS, ANPs, commercial Lipofectamine 3000 alone (Lipo), ANP‐derived lipid shell (Shell), heat‐inactivated ANPs, ANP‐derived RNA-loaded liposomes (RNA), or metabolite‐loaded liposomes (Metabolite). Arrows indicate cells exhibiting activation-associated morphological changes. Scale bars, 100 μm (B) Representative flow cytometry histograms of CD86, MHC‐I, and TNF‐α expression in RAW 264.7 macrophages treated with individual ANP cargo components. (C) Schematic overview of ANP‐RNA generation. The lipid fraction and total RNA were independently isolated from ANPs and subsequently reconstituted into RNA‐reloaded nanoparticles using a standard encapsulation procedure. (D) RT-qPCR analysi

### 2.5. Clinical relevance and synergistic αPD-1 efficacy of ANPs
To explore the clinical relevance of macrophage state regulation associated with the ANP-miR-488/ BCL6 axis, human single-cell and bulk transcriptomes were integrated. A macrophage-enriched signature was defined using lung adenocarcinoma (LUAD) single-cell data ( GSE131907 ) ( Fig. 6 A). This signature was then applied to LUAD and lung squamous cell carcinoma cohorts to stratify macrophage-enriched patient samples, which were further classified into BCL6 high and BCL6 low groups ( Fig. S34 ). The BCL6 low group exhibited a pronounced immune-inflamed phenotype, characterized by elevated expression of M1-associated inflammatory markers ( TNF, CD80, CD86, CXCL9 , and CXCL10 ), whereas immunosuppressive markers ( ARG1, CD163 ) were reduced or unchanged ( Fig. 6 B). Moreover, CD8 + T cell infiltration ( CD8A, CD8B, CXCR3 ) and effector activation signatures ( IFNG, GZMB, PRF1 ) were markedly upregulated in the BCL6 low group ( Fig. 6 C), recapitulating the macrophage-T cell crosstalk observed in our experimental models.
Clinical relevance of ANPs associated with BCL6-dependent immune remodeling and enhanced response to αPD-1 therapy. (A) Identification of a macrophage-enriched gene signature using the GSE131907 lung adenocarcinoma (LUAD) single-cell RNA-sequencing dataset. (B, C) Transcriptomic analysis of macrophage-enriched TCGA lung cancer cohorts (LUAD and LUSC) stratified into BCL6 high and BCL6 low groups. (B) Violin plots showing the expression of M1-associated inflammatory markers ( TNF , CD80 , CD86 , CXCL9 , CXCL10 ) and immunosuppressive markers ( ARG1 , CD163 ). (C) Violin plots showing genes associated with CD8 + T cell infiltration ( CD8A , CD8B , CXCR3 ) and cytotoxic effector molecules ( IFNG , GZMB , PRF1 ). (D) Association between BCL6 expression and clinical response to αPD-1 therapy in the GSE299686 cohort. Macrophage-enriched patients were stratified by BCL6 expression level, and the proportions of responders versus non-responders were compared. (E) 

### 3. Conclusion
In this study, we establish a systematic discovery framework for bioactive plant-derived nanoparticles (PNPs) that moves beyond the conventional one-plant-one-function paradigm. By constructing and physicochemically mapping a taxonomically diverse library of 110 PNPs, we demonstrate that biological functionality can be quantitatively assessed through functional screening, enabling unbiased identification of functional bio-nanomaterials from natural diversity. Using this approach, ANPs were identified as a previously unrecognized functional PNP with pronounced immunomodulatory activity, emerging from systematic screening rather than predefined molecular design.
Mechanistic analyses revealed that ANP bioactivity is primarily associated with RNA cargo carried by the PNPs. In particular, ANP-miR-488 suppresses the transcriptional regulator Bcl6, establishing a clear structure-function relationship that links nanoscale carrier architecture to immune modulation. These findings suggest that RNA functions as a key active component contributing to the immunomodulatory activity of ANPs.

### Ethics approval and consent to participate
All animal procedures were conducted in accordance with protocols approved by the Kyungpook National University Institutional Animal Care and Use Committee (IACUC; approval number: KNU-2025-0783).
PBMCs from healthy donors were obtained from Kyungpook National University Medical Center (KNUMC). Blood collection was approved by the Institutional Review Board of KNUMC (Approval No. 2022-02-011), and written informed consent was obtained from all donors.
